2. **`Removed()` returning false on `Getsockname` error.** If `Getsockname` errors (e.g., EBADF after probe closed), returns false. Probe is closed in `Close()`; but `reopen` calls `failed.Close()` via defer, then later calls `failed.Removed()`? Let's check ordering: `reopen` does `defer failed.Close()` — the defer runs when reopen returns, AFTER `gone()` is evaluated in both exit paths (ctx.Done and successful open). So `gone()` is called before `Close`. Good.

But wait — in the success path: `cause := gone()` → `failed.Removed()` is called while `failed` socket is still open. Good.

However, there's a subtle resource issue: `defer failed.Close()` runs when reopen returns. In `run`, after reopen returns non-nil, the old source was already closed by reopen — good, no double close (previously run closed then reopen reopened; now reopen closes). And if reopen returns nil (ctx done), `run` returns; the deferred close in `run` closes `source` — wait, `run`'s defer closes `source`, which is the local variable... In `run`:

```go
defer func() {
    if source != nil {
        source.Close()
    }
}()
...
source = r.reopen(ctx, source)
```

Hmm, the deferred closure captures the variable `source`, so when reopen returns nil and run returns, the deferred close runs on the updated `source` — nil, so skipped. And reopen already closed the failed one via defer. Good, single close.

Previously: run closed source before calling reopen; reopen returned new source; deferred close closes new source. Equivalent.

But careful: `run`'s deferred closure references `source` by reference (closure over variable) — in the new code, after `source = r.reopen(ctx, source)`, if reopen returns a new source and then run continues... eventually run's defer closes the current source. Fine.

3. **Double close of probe:** `afpacketSource.Close()` calls `unix.Close(s.probe)` unconditionally. If Close is called twice, `unix.Close(-1?)` — probe is a valid fd; double-close would close an fd that may have been reused by another goroutine → dangerous. Is Close ever called twice on the same source? In `run`, defer closes current source; reopen closes the failed one. The failed source and current source are distinct objects. In the error path of `openPacketSockets`, sources opened so far get Closed once. In `run`'s NewOutput failure path... sources closed once. Each source gets closed exactly once in normal flow. But note `defer failed.Close()` in reopen plus run's deferred `source.Close()`: when reopen returns nil and run returns, `source` variable was set to nil before return — wait: `source = r.reopen(ctx, source)`; reopen returns nil, so `source = nil`, run returns, defer checks nil → skip. OK.

But there's one path: in `run`, after reopen returns nil, `return` — the deferred close skipped. Good.

What about `Removed()` being called on a closed probe (Getsockname on closed fd → EBADF, returns false)? In `reopen`, `gone()` is invoked before `defer failed.Close()` executes (defers run at return). So fine. But could `Removed()` be called on a source whose probe fd was closed and the fd reused by another goroutine opening a new source? Only if Removed is called after Close; flow doesn't do that. OK.

4. **Race on ifindex:** `openRemovalProbe` uses `net.InterfaceByName(name)` to get the index. There's a TOCTOU: interface could be removed between the afpacket handle creation and InterfaceByName. Then `net.InterfaceByName` fails → openPacketSocket closes handle and returns error. Fine.

But another issue: `net.InterfaceByName` operates in the *current* network namespace, while the afpacket handle was opened... `openPacketSockets` runs `exec` to enter the node's namespace, then opens sockets. So `net.InterfaceByName` inside that exec closure runs in the node namespace. Good — `openRemovalProbe` is called from `openPacketSocket`, which is invoked within the exec closure. Good.

However: `iface.Index` vs the afpacket handle's actual bound interface. `afpacket.NewTPacket(OptInterface(name))` resolves the interface by name at open time; if the interface is recreated between the handle open and InterfaceByName, the index could differ. Minor race; index would then be recorded as the new interface's index — probing then compares against wrong baseline. Extremely unlikely; not worth flagging maybe. Actually there's a subtle correctness issue: if the interface is removed and recreated with the SAME index (possible? ifindex allocation is monotonic per-namespace, so a recreated interface gets a new ifindex; indices aren't reused until wraparound). So recreated interface has different ifindex → Removed correctly returns true. Good design.

5. **The gap reason wording:** `reportGap` produces `"the interface was not available: %v"` with cause `errInterfaceRemoved` = "it was removed from its network namespace". So reason becomes "the interface was not available: it was removed from its network namespace". Slightly odd phrasing but fine.

6. **Bug candidate — the `gone()` closure and the timeout path:** When reopen succeeds and `gone()` returns `errInterfaceRemoved`, reportGap(from, now, cause). Fine.

When ctx is done and reopen exits with `unavailable` set — reports gap with `To` zero (in-progress). Fine, matches prior behavior.

When ctx is done and neither unavailable nor removed — no gap. Fine.

7. **`Removed()` semantics for moved interfaces:** OK.

8. **Potential correctness issue: `Removed()` compares `link.Ifindex != s.ifindex`.** For AF_PACKET, `getsockname` returns `sll_ifindex = po->ifindex`. Initially bound to `iface.Index`. Yes, returns po->ifindex. Fine.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: `SockaddrLinklayer` returned by `unix.Getsockname` — for AF_PACKET, the syscall returns `sockaddr_ll` with `sll_family`, `sll_protocol`, `sll_ifindex`, `sll_hatype`, `sll_pkttype`, `sll_halen`, `sll_addr`. Go's unix package parses it into `SockaddrLinklayer`. OK.

Edge: When the interface is removed, `po->ifindex` becomes -1 (per PR claim). Then `Removed()` returns true. When interface only goes down, ifindex stays. Good.

But what if the interface is removed and a NEW interface is created that reuses... not possible per monotonic ifindex within a namespace (per-net index counter increments; only wraps after INT_MAX). Fine.

9. **Missed gap when interface removed but reopen keeps failing (unavailable != nil).** `gone()` prioritizes `unavailable` over removal — reports the open failure as the cause, not the removal. That's fine/acceptable — the gap reason reflects the most recent info.

What about: interface removed, but reopen attempts fail a few times with "no such interface" (unavailable set), then succeed. `gone()` returns `unavailable` — reported as "not available", not as removed. Consistent with old behavior. Fine.

10. **Down-then-removed with no reopen failure:** Interface goes down → socket read error → reopen. Meanwhile interface removed (e.g., node destroyed) and recreated: reopen succeeds quickly. `failed.Removed()` true → gap reported. Good — that's the fix's purpose: previously, a fast restart that finished before the first reopen attempt was not reported as a gap at all (since unavailable never set). Now it is. That's the PR's purpose.

But `gone()`/`reportGap` happen after `waitOpened` returns... there is a race: the test waits for opened gen 1, then waits for `closed` of gen 0 — that's closed by the defer in reopen, which runs when reopen returns, which happens AFTER `gone()` evaluation and reportGap. 26. **Now check the removed/changed semantics in `run`:** Previously `source.Close()` before reopen — meaning the failed socket was closed before attempting reopen. Now `failed.Close()` happens via defer when reopen returns — meaning during the whole reopen loop, the failed source's fd stays open (the probe AND the handle). Impact: while the node is down/missing, the old ring buffer memory (8 MiB per source) is held longer. Also, holding the old handle open while reopening: the afpacket handle is bound to the old (removed) interface — it's dead anyway. Holding it during reopen keeps the 8-block ring mapped. Minor memory cost during outage; not a big deal. But also: does holding the old socket matter for correctness? No.

Actually wait — there IS a subtle behavioral point: previously the socket was closed immediately upon error, freeing resources during potentially long outages. Now it stays open until reopen succeeds or ctx done. For a long outage (node destroyed and never comes back until ctx ends), the ring (8 MiB) and two fds stay. Minor. Not flagging as a defect; maybe P4? Probably not even worth it. Hmm. It's a real but minor resource-holding change. The kernel keeps the socket alive anyway since we still had it. I might mention in coverage notes rather than a finding.

```go
func OptInterface(name string) Opt {
```

  Line 24: ## Packet capture with `containerlab capture`

  Line 26: The new [`capture`](../cmd/capture.md) command records packets from one or more lab link interfaces into a single pcapng file, or streams them live into Wireshark. It works with Docker, Podman and c9s, without installing capture tools in the nodes:

  Line 30: containerlab capture --name srl \

  Line 33: # capture two interfaces for 30 seconds

  Line 34: containerlab capture -t srl.clab.yml \

  Line 37: # list capturable interfaces and their peers

  Line 38: containerlab capture --name srl --list

  Line 41: Interfaces can be selected by their name or topology alias, and every packet is associated with its `<node>:<interface>` name in the capture. Packets from all targets are merged in timestamp order. Captures can be bounded by duration or packet count, truncated to a snap length, and stopped cleanly with Ctrl+C while leaving a valid pcapng stream.

  Line 43: With Docker and Podman, containerlab captures through packet sockets in the node network namespaces; the command needs root, like `deploy`, but neither the host nor the node needs `tcpdump`. VLAN tags are preserved, interfaces that go down and back up remain captured, and a node restart is reattached with the missed interval reported as a gap. With c9s, the `clabwire` sidecar captures the packets, and long captures are chained without duplicating packets at the handover.

  Line 52: The c9s runtime now requires clabernetes 0.11 or newer, which provides Node run state and the clabwire packet-capture contract. Containerlab checks the cluster's published Node schema before the first cluster operation and reports a clear error for older releases. Offline commands such as `validate`, `deploy --emit-crs` and file-based `graph` do not need cluster access.

  Line 81: Containerlab's Go API gained lab-runtime interfaces for locating nodes, opening interactive terminals, streaming logs, forwarding TCP ports, sampling CPU and memory usage, and capturing packets. The c9s implementation supports Kubernetes user impersonation per runtime or per call, applying the selected identity to REST requests, watches, logs, exec and port-forward connections. These APIs let services such as clab-api-server provide node access while Kubernetes RBAC continues to authorize each user.

/source/head/docs/cmd/capture.md:
  Line 1: # capture command

  Line 5: The `capture` command captures packets on lab link interfaces of one or more nodes and writes them as one [pcapng](https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-opsawg-pcapng-03.html) stream, to a file or to stdout for Wireshark.

  Line 9: - **Docker and Podman**: containerlab opens a packet socket on each interface inside the node's network namespace. Nothing runs in the node, the node image needs no capture tools, and no extra software is needed on the host: the command needs root, like `deploy`. See [How Docker and Podman captures work](#how-docker-and-podman-captures-work).

  Line 10: - **c9s**: the `clabwire` sidecar of each node's pod captures the packets; see the [c9s runtime](../manual/clabernetes/runtime.md#packet-capture).

  Line 18: `containerlab [global-flags] capture [local-flags]`

  Line 26: With Docker and Podman, the capturable interfaces are the links of the topology file, so `--name` needs the topology file the lab was deployed from to still be in place.

  Line 30: The local `--target` flag selects what to capture, as `<node>:<interface>[,<interface>...]`. Repeat it for more nodes:

  Line 38: Only lab link interfaces can be captured; the management interface cannot. An interface that is not a link interface of the node fails the command before anything is captured, and the error lists the node's capturable interfaces.

  Line 42: The local `--duration` flag stops the capture after the given time (`30s`, `5m`, `2h`). The default `0` captures until the command is interrupted with Ctrl+C or SIGTERM. Captures may run longer than an hour; see [Long captures](#long-captures).

  Line 46: The local `--packet-limit` flag stops the capture after that many packets, counted across all targets. `0`, the default, means no limit.

  Line 58: The local `--list` flag lists the interfaces `capture` accepts, for every running node, with the node and interface at the other end of each link, instead of capturing. `--format | -f` selects `table` (default) or `json`.

  Line 68: The interface name shows in Wireshark's interface column (`frame.interface_name`), so a capture of several nodes can be filtered by node and interface.

  Line 70: When the capture ends (duration, packet limit, Ctrl+C or SIGTERM), the command finishes the stream and exits with code 0; the file is a valid capture. Status messages go to stderr. With c9s, every `clabwire` capture in the pods ends with the command: containerlab closes its stdin, and `clabwire` (`--stop-on-stdin-close`) stops.

  Line 88: ### How Docker and Podman captures work

  Line 90: For each target, containerlab enters the node's network namespace, opens a Linux packet socket (`AF_PACKET`) bound to the interface, and leaves the namespace again; the socket keeps capturing on that interface. This is what `tcpdump` does inside the node, without needing `tcpdump` in the node or on the host. All sockets are opened before the first byte is written, so a capture that cannot start (a node that does not run, a missing interface) fails without output.

  Line 94: - Each socket is in promiscuous mode for as long as it is open; the interface returns to its previous state when the capture ends.

  Line 95: - An interface that goes down and up again keeps being captured. When an interface disappears, for example while its node restarts, containerlab waits for it and reattaches once the node is back (`containerlab restart --node`). The time it was gone is reported as a warning with its start and end time.

  Line 99: ### Long captures

  Line 101: Docker and Podman captures have no length limit. With c9s, one `clabwire` capture lasts at most one hour. `capture` chains captures: it starts the next one shortly before the current one ends, lets both run for a few seconds, and switches between them in the middle of that overlap. The repeated section and interface headers of the follow-up are dropped, and packets seen by both captures near the switch are written once (they are matched by content, because each capture stamps packets when it reads them). Once the switch time has passed, the previous capture is stopped through its stdin instead of running to its end. If a follow-up capture does not start in time, the missing interval is reported as a warning with its start and end time; it is never skipped silently. The timestamp order holds across switches: while one node's capture switches, packets of other nodes stamped after the switch wait for it.

  Line 108: containerlab capture -t srl.clab.yml \

  Line 112: #### Watch a capture live in Wireshark

  Line 115: containerlab capture --name srl --target srl1:e1-1,e1-2 -w - | wireshark -k -i -

  Line 121: containerlab capture --name srl --target srl1:ethernet-1/1 --packet-limit 100 -w - | tshark -r -

  Line 124: #### List the capturable interfaces

  Line 127: containerlab capture --name srl --list

  Line 133: containerlab -r c9s capture --name srl --target srl1:e1-1 --target srl2:e1-1 -w srl.pcapng

/source/head/docs/manual/wireshark.md:
  Line 4:   - Packet capture

  Line 8: # Packet capture & Wireshark

  Line 10: Every lab emulation software must provide its users with the packet capturing abilities. Looking at the frames as they traverse the network links is not only educational, but also helps to troubleshoot the issues that might arise during the lab development.

  Line 16: Containerlab offers a simple way to capture the packets from any interface of any node in the lab. This article will explain how to do that.

  Line 20: The [`containerlab capture`](../cmd/capture.md) command captures one or more lab link interfaces, of one or more nodes, into one pcapng file or stream, with nothing to install in the nodes or on the host:

  Line 23: containerlab capture --name quickstart --target srl:e1-1 -w - | wireshark -k -i -

  Line 26: If you are looking for a free Web UI for packet capture with Wireshark, check out our [Edgeshark integration](#edgeshark-integration).

  Line 29: Consider the following lab topology which highlights the typical points of packet capture.

  Line 34: Since containerlab leverages Linux network devices, users can use any tool to sniff packets. Let's see how one can use either or a combination of the following well-known packet capturing tools: `tcpdump`, `tshark` and `wireshark`.

  Line 36: ## Packet capture, namespaces and interfaces

  Line 38: To capture the packets from a given interface requires having that interface's name and its network namespace (netns). And that's it.

  Line 53: Now when it is clear which netns names corresponds to which container and which interfaces are available inside the given lab node, it's easy to start capturing traffic. But how to do that?

  Line 55: There are two most common ways of capturing the traffic:

  Line 57: - local capture: when the capture is started from the containerlab host itself.

  Line 58: - remote capture: when the capture is started from a remote machine that connects via SSH to the containerlab host and starts the capture.

  Line 60: In both cases, the capturing software (`tcpdump` or `tshark`) needs to be available on the containerlab host.

  Line 62: ### local capture

  Line 64: Local capture assumes the capture is initiated from the containerlab host. For instance, to capture from the `e1-1` interface of the `clab-quickstart-srl` node use:

  Line 70: In this example we first entered the namespace where the target interface is located using `ip netns exec` command and then started the capture with `tcpdump` providing the interface name to it.

  Line 72: The downside of local capture is that typically containerlab hosts run in a headless (no UI) mode and thus the visibility of the captured traffic is limited to the console output. This is where `tshark` might come in in handy by providing more readable output. Still, the lack of Wireshark UI is a downside, therefore it is our recommendation for you to get familiar with the remote capture method.

  Line 74: ### remote capture

  Line 76: The limitations of the local capture are lifted when the remote capture is used. In the remote capture model you initiate the packet capture from your descktop/laptop and the traffic is sent to your machine where it can be displayed in the Wireshark UI.

  Line 78: Before we start mixing in the Wireshark, lets see how the remote capture is initiated:

  Line 85: Assuming we ran the above command from our laptop, we rely on `$containerlab_host_address` being reachable from our laptop. We use `ssh` to connect to the remote containerlab host and execute the same command we did in the local capture.  

  Line 87: But simply seeing the tcpdump output on your laptop's terminal doesn't offer much difference to the local capture.

  Line 89: The true power the remote capture has is in being able to use the Wireshark installed on your machine to display the captured traffic. To do that we need to pipe the output of the `tcpdump` command to the `wireshark` command. This is done by adding the `-w -` option to the `tcpdump` command which tells it to write the captured traffic to the standard output. The output is then piped to the `wireshark` command which is invoked with the `-k -i -` options. The `-k` option tells wireshark to start capturing immediately and the `-i -` option tells it to read the input from the standard input.

  Line 97: This will start the capture from a given interface and redirect the received flow to the Wireshark!

  Line 116: Since capturing is a so popular it makes sense to create a tiny helper script that will simplify the process of capturing from a given interface of a given node. The script presented below hides all the irrelevant details and makes sniffing a breeze. Let's see how it works:

  Line 145: - `srl` is the container name of the node that has the interface you want to capture from

  Line 146: - `e1-1` is the interface name you want to capture from

  Line 148: The script uses the `tshark` CLI tool instead of `tcpdump` to be able to capture from multiple interfaces at once[^1]. This is achieved by splitting the interface names by comma and passing them to the `tshark` command as `-i <interface1> -i <interface2> -i <interface3>`.

  Line 162: The [capture script](#capture-script) already makes it easy to dump packets off of an interface and piping it to a Wireshark UI. Is there anything that can make it even easier?

  Line 172: [Edgeshark][edgeshark-docs] visualizes the communication of containers and thus helps in diagnosing it, both in-between containers as well as with the "outside world". It can be deployed to Linux stand-alone container hosts, including KinD deployments. Edgeshark also supports capturing container traffic using Wireshark.

  Line 175: Yep, you got it right, edgeshark is a Web UI for Wireshark[^2] that is capable of capturing traffic from any interface of any container (and physical interface) in your lab. Moreover, it plugs into containerlab natively, and is free and open-source.

  Line 225: 2. Installing the external capture plugin for Wireshark.

  Line 227: Luckily, you only need to do it once and it will work for all the future captures on any system you install EdgeShark on.

  Line 229: [Edgeshark documentation](https://edgeshark.siemens.io/#/getting-started?id=optional-capture-plugin) provides a detailed guide on how to perform these two steps for Windows and Linux systems.

  Line 243: 2. Download the [external capture plugin](https://github.com/siemens/cshargextcap/releases/latest) for Darwin OS and your architecture, unarchive and copy it with Finder[^4] to the `/Applications/Wireshark.app/Contents/MacOS/extcap/` directory.

  Line 245: With these steps done[^5], you should be able to click on the "fin" icon next to the interface name and see the Wireshark UI opening up and starting the capture.

  Line 249: The [Containerlab GUI](gui/index.md#packet-capture) exposes packet capture from interface actions. In VS Code, the capture action can open a Wireshark VNC session in a VS Code webview, avoiding local extcap plugin and `packetflix://` handler setup for that workflow.

This is what `tcpdump` does inside the node, without needing `tcpdump` in the node or on the host. All sockets are opened before the first byte is written, so a capture that cannot start (a node that does not run, a missing interface) fails without output.\n\n  Line 94: - Each socket is in promiscuous mode for as long as it is open; the interface returns to its previous state when the capture ends.\n\n  Line 95: - An interface that goes down and up again keeps being captured. When an interface disappears, for example while its node restarts, containerlab waits for it and reattaches once the node is back (`containerlab restart --node`). The time it was gone is reported as a warning with its start and end time.\n\n  Line 99: ### Long captures\n\n  Line 101: Docker and Podman captures have no length limit. With c9s, one `clabwire` capture lasts at most one hour. `capture` chains captures: it starts the next one shortly before the current one ends, lets both run for a few seconds, and switches between them in the middle of that overlap. The repeated section and interface headers of the follow-up are dropped, and packets seen by both captures near the switch are written once (they are matched by content, because each capture stamps packets when it reads them). Once the switch time has passed, the previous capture is stopped through its stdin instead of running to its end. If a follow-up capture does not start in time, the missing interval is reported as a warning with its start and end time; it is never skipped silently. The timestamp order holds across switches: while one node's capture switches, packets of other nodes stamped after the switch wait for it.\n\n  Line 108: containerlab capture -t srl.clab.yml \\\n\n  Line 112: #### Watch a capture live in Wireshark\n\n  Line 115: containerlab capture --name srl --target srl1:e1-1,e1-2 -w - | wireshark -k -i -\n\n  Line 121: containerlab capture --name srl --target srl1:ethernet-1/1 --packet-limit 100 -w - | tshark -r -\n\n  Line 124: #### List the capturable interfaces\n\n  Line 127: containerlab capture --name srl --list\n\n  Line 133: containerlab -r c9s capture --name srl --target srl1:e1-1 --target srl2:e1-1 -w srl.pcapng\n\n\n/source/head/docs/manual/kinds/rare-freertr.md:\n  Line 160: * `pcap` folder is where `pcap` files are located (`packet capture eth1`)\n\n\n/source/head/docs/manual/kinds/sros.md:\n  Line 747: Currently, a packet capture on the veth interfaces of the `-{{ kind_display_name }}-` will only display traffic at the ingress direction[^10]. step-start
{
  "type": "step-start",
  "id": "prt_1f99eb97c00191tmA4lyDNjmef",
  "sessionID": "ses_e0662e07bffe3CRZbqck2Jutxp",
  "messageID": "msg_1f99eaff2001ake4tezEsbl5X6"

Reasoning
No vendor dir. I can't verify kernel behavior from source here; but the described kernel behavior (AF_PACKET sockets bound to an interface that gets unregistered get `po->ifindex = -1`, visible via `getsockname`) is a known, real behavior in net/packet/af_packet.c's `packet_notifier` (NETDEV_UNREGISTER resets `po->ifindex` to -1). I'm reasonably confident this is correct. This is also why tools like tcpdump detect interface removal.

Now let me consider correctness issues more carefully:

**Issue A: `Removed()` result depends on which side of an interface rename... no.**

**Issue B: The probe socket's ifindex baseline vs the afpacket handle's binding.** If between `NewTPacket(OptInterface(name))` and `net.InterfaceByName(name)` the interface is deleted and re-added, the probe binds to the NEW ifindex while the handle holds the OLD one. Then `Removed()` returns false forever for the old removed interface; the handle never receives packets; the reader spins? Actually handle read would error out (interface gone → poll returns error?) — likely the handle's read errors, triggering reopen each time... reopen then fails with "no such interface" (or succeeds on the new one). This is a tiny race with self-correcting behavior. Not worth flagging.

**Issue C: Down vs removed for a restart with the SAME interface recreated with the SAME ifindex?** ifindex allocation is monotonic per netns (`net->ifindex` counter), so a recreated interface gets a fresh ifindex. Except netns re-creation: if the whole netns is destroyed and recreated, ifindex counters start over — a node restart destroys the netns (new nsid? actually the container's netns is a new netns), so a restarted node's netns is new and ifindex can start from 2 again (lo=1, eth0=2...). But the probe socket belonged to the OLD netns — once the netns is destroyed, the socket's ifindex is -1 (device unregistered before netns teardown). So Removed still works. Good.

**Issue D: `unix.Close(s.probe)` double-close risk.** As analyzed, each source is closed exactly once in the current flow. 	case NETDEV_UNREGISTER:
```c
		if (po->mclist)
			packet_dev_mc(dev, po->mclist, -1);
		fallthrough;
	case NETDEV_DOWN:
		spin_lock(&po->bind_lock);
		if (po->running)
			unregister_prot_hook(sk, false);
		if (msg == NETDEV_UNREGISTER) {
			packet_cached_dev_reset(po);

		break;
```

Coverage notes:
- Reviewed both changed paths fully against base; traced `packetSource` implementers (`afpacketSource`, test fake), `ReportGap`, `CaptureLocal` callers via `localCapture.run`.
- Docs: `docs/cmd/capture.md` and `docs/rn/0.83.md` already claim that the time an interface is gone is reported as a warning; the change makes the implementation match that claim for fast restarts, so no doc change is needed.
- Schema: no topology API change; `schemas/clab.schema.json` unaffected.
- Kernel behavior assumed: `getsockname()` on an `AF_PACKET` socket bound to an interface returns `Ifindex == -1` after that interface is unregistered (deleted/moved/leaves the netns) and keeps the bound index when the interface merely goes down; this could not be verified against kernel source in this environment, and the new unit test exercises the Go logic with a fake source rather than the kernel path.

/source/head/docs/cmd/capture.md:
  Line 95: - An interface that goes down and up again keeps being captured. When an interface disappears, for example while its node restarts, containerlab waits for it and reattaches once the node is back (`containerlab restart --node`). The time it was gone is reported as a warning with its start and end time.

  Line 101: Docker and Podman captures have no length limit. With c9s, one `clabwire` capture lasts at most one hour. `capture` chains captures: it starts the next one shortly before the current one ends, lets both run for a few seconds, and switches between them in the middle of that overlap. The repeated section and interface headers of the follow-up are dropped, and packets seen by both captures near the switch are written once (they are matched by content, because each capture stamps packets when it reads them). Once the switch time has passed, the previous capture is stopped through its stdin instead of running to its end. If a follow-up capture does not start in time, the missing interval is reported as a warning with its start and end time; it is never skipped silently. The timestamp order holds across switches: while one node's capture switches, packets of other nodes stamped after the switch wait for it.

Summary of analysis:
- The PR adds a per-source removal probe: a protocol-0 `AF_PACKET` socket bound to the interface, opened inside the node's netns during `openPacketSocket`. `Removed()` reads back the bound ifindex via `getsockname`; the kernel resets a packet socket's bound ifindex to `-1` when its device is unregistered (deleted / moved out of the netns), while a mere link-down keeps it. That distinguishes a fast node restart (interface back before the first reopen attempt, so `unavailable` was never set and the base code reported no gap at all) from a mere link flap.
- Ownership: `reopen` now closes the failed source via `defer`, eliminating the prior split (`run` closed, then reopened). Verified single-close per source in all paths: `run`'s deferred close skips nil, `openAll`/`NewOutput` failure paths close once.
- The probe approach is the minimal correct one: the reader goroutine lives in the host netns, so any netlink/`net.InterfaceByIndex` check would query the wrong namespace; only a socket created inside the node's netns can report the binding state of that namespace.
- Docs already claim gap reporting on interface disappearance (`docs/cmd/capture.md` line 95, `docs/rn/0.83.md` line 43); the change makes the implementation match that claim rather than require doc edits. No schema impact.
- Tests: new test covers the fast-restart gap; existing tests confirm down-without-gap and restart-with-unavailable-gap still hold.
- One P4 wording nit on the `errInterfaceRemoved` comment.
# Code Review: local capture — report interfaces that left the failed socket's namespace

## Summary

This PR fixes a gap-reporting blind spot in the local (Docker/Podman) packet capture: when a node restarts so fast that its interface is already back by the first reopen attempt, `unavailable` was never set and the missed interval was silently unreported. The fix gives each `afpacketSource` a "probe" socket — an `AF_PACKET` socket bound to the interface with protocol 0, opened inside the node's netns — and asks it at reopen end whether the interface left the failed source's namespace. Ownership of the failed source moves into `reopen` (`defer failed.Close()`), which keeps the close exactly-once in all exit paths I traced (`run`'s deferred close skips `nil`, `openAll`/`NewOutput` failure paths close once, `reopen` closes the failed source exactly once).

The design is sound and minimal: the reader goroutine lives in the host netns, so any host-side check (`net.InterfaceByIndex`, netlink) would query the wrong namespace; only a socket created inside the node's netns can report that namespace's binding state. Ifindex allocation is monotonic per netns, so a recreated interface cannot alias the recorded index, and a whole-destroyed netns still yields `-1` because device unregistration precedes netns teardown. The probe does not enable promiscuous mode and receives nothing (protocol 0 registers no packet handler), so there is no behavioral side effect on the captured interface.

Docs need no change: `docs/cmd/capture.md` ("The time it was gone is reported as a warning with its start and end time") and `docs/rn/0.83.md` already state the behavior this PR implements; the change makes the implementation match that claim for fast restarts. There is no topology/config API change, so `schemas/clab.schema.json` is unaffected. The new test covers the fixed scenario, and the two existing reopen tests confirm the down-without-gap and restart-with-`unavailable`-gap behaviors still hold. `golang.org/x/sys` is already a direct dependency (`go.mod` v0.49.0).

## Findings

| Priority | Count |
|----------|-------|
| P0 | 0 |
| P1 | 0 |
| P2 | 0 |
| P3 | 0 |
| P4 | 1 |

### P4 — Clarify the `errInterfaceRemoved` doc comment

**Location:** [capture/local.go, lines 50–51](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/ea9c5e8bd08d161744c213960668476a15e35d36/capture/local.go#L50-L51) (head)

**Problem:** The comment added by this PR reads "`errInterfaceRemoved` is why an interface that was back by the first reopen was gone." This is hard to parse on first read: "back by the first reopen" reads like "restored by" rather than "back before", and the sentence does not say what the error is used for. The full explanation only becomes clear after reading the `reopen` doc comment ([capture/local.go, lines 310–314](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/ea9c5e8bd08d161744c213960668476a15e35d36/capture/local.go#L310-L314)) and the probe comment.

**Trigger:** A reader of `capture/local.go` encountering the error declaration before `reopen`.

**Impact:** Small maintenance cost only — the code behavior is correct; the declaration just does not explain itself where it sits.

**Correction:** Reword the comment to state purpose and trigger, for example: "`errInterfaceRemoved` reports that the interface left the network namespace of the failed socket, which a restart that is already over when the first reopen attempt runs does." The assembled gap reason ("the interface was not available: it was removed from its network namespace") is acceptable as is; no change needed there.

## Coverage notes

- Both changed paths (`capture/local.go`, `capture/local_test.go`) were reviewed in full against the base revision, including the diff hunks and surrounding code.
- Traced all callers and implementers of the changed `packetSource` interface: only `afpacketSource` and the test `fakeSource` implement it, both updated. Verified close-once ownership across `run`, `reopen`, `openAll`, and the `NewOutput` failure path; no double-close or leak found, and the probe fd is closed on its own error paths.
- Verified the reported-gap orderings: `gone()` (and thus `Removed()`) is evaluated before `defer failed.Close()` runs in both exit paths, so the probe socket is always alive when consulted; the new test's synchronization (close observation then mutex-guarded gap read) is sound.
- Docs: `docs/cmd/capture.md` line 95 and `docs/rn/0.83.md` line 43 already document that the time an interface is gone is reported; this PR closes the implementation gap for fast restarts, so no doc edit is required. Schema: not in scope — no topology API change.
- The kernel-side claim behind the probe (an `AF_PACKET` socket's bound ifindex read back via `getsockname` becomes `-1` when its device is unregistered/deleted/moved, and stays unchanged on mere link-down) follows the behavior described in the PR comment and is consistent with `net/packet/af_packet.c`'s notifier behavior, but it could not be verified against kernel source in this environment; the new unit test exercises the Go logic against a fake, not the kernel path. The pre-existing base code already relied on the related assumption that a removed interface's socket reports an error.
- One resource note, not a defect: the failed source (8 MiB ring plus probe fd) is now held for the whole reopen wait instead of being closed immediately at error time. This is inherent to consulting `Removed()` at reopen end, and the cost is bounded by the number of capture targets.
- Tests were not executed (source-only review); formatting was inspected by reading and appears `gofmt`-clean, with no supplied CI evidence to cite.
